## [HN-TITLE] 15. A digestion of the Jacobian conjecture counterexample

- **Source**: [https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2026/07/21/a-digestion-of-the-jacobian-conjecture-counterexample/](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2026/07/21/a-digestion-of-the-jacobian-conjecture-counterexample/)
- **Site**: What's new
- **Author**: Terence Tao
- **Published**: 2026-07-21
- **HN activity**: 275 points · [112 comments](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48998362)
- **Length**: 2.3K words (~11 min read)
- **Language**: en

The notorious [Jacobian conjecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobian_conjecture) can be formulated concretely over the complex numbers as follows.

> **Conjecture 1 (Jacobian Conjecture)** Let ![{F:{\bf C}^n \rightarrow {\bf C}^n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%3A%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5En%20%5Crightarrow%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5En%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a polynomial map in ![{n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) complex variables, whose Jacobian ![{\mathrm{det} DF}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7Bdet%7D%20DF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a non-zero constant. Then ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is invertible (with polynomial inverse).

The condition that the Jacobian ![{\mathrm{det} DF}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7Bdet%7D%20DF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is non-zero is equivalent to ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) being locally invertible. (The implication of local invertibility from non-vanishing Jacobian follows from the inverse function theorem; the converse implication can be derived from the [Weierstrass preparation theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weierstrass_preparation_theorem), but is omitted here.) Also, from the fundamental theorem of algebra, once the Jacobian polynomial ![{\mathrm{det} DF}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7Bdet%7D%20DF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is non-zero, it must be constant. So the hypothesis “Jacobian ![{\mathrm{det} DF}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7Bdet%7D%20DF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a non-zero constant” can be replaced with “![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is locally invertible”. So the Jacobian conjecture can be viewed as an assertion that local invertibility implies global invertibility. The complex numbers can be easily replaced with other fields of characteristic zero by the [Lefschetz principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algebraic_geometry_and_analytic_geometry#The_Lefschetz_principle), but I prefer to work in the concrete setting of the complex numbers.

It was [recently shown](https://www.newscientist.com/article/2580374-ais-solution-to-87-year-old-riddle-takes-mathematicians-by-surprise/) (using the Fable AI) that the conjecture is false in three dimensions (and thus in higher dimensions as well):

> **Theorem 2 (Counterexample to conjecture)** []()There exists a polynomial ![{F : {\bf C}^3 \rightarrow {\bf C}^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%20%3A%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%20%5Crightarrow%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) which has non-zero constant Jacobian, but is not invertible.

The conjecture remains open in two dimensions, and is easy to establish in one dimension.

The example can be stated completely explicitly: one can take[]()

[![\displaystyle  F(z_1,z_2,z_3) = \Big((1+z_1 z_2)^3 z_3 + z_2^2 (1+z_1z_2) (4+3z_1z_2), \ \ \ \ \ (1)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20F%28z_1%2Cz_2%2Cz_3%29%20%3D%20%5CBig%28%281%2Bz_1%20z_2%29%5E3%20z_3%20%2B%20z_2%5E2%20%281%2Bz_1z_2%29%20%284%2B3z_1z_2%29%2C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%281%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

![\displaystyle  z_2 + 3 z_1 (1+z_1z_2)^2 z_3 + 3 z_1 z_2^2 (4+3z_1z_2), ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20z_2%20%2B%203%20z_1%20%281%2Bz_1z_2%29%5E2%20z_3%20%2B%203%20z_1%20z_2%5E2%20%284%2B3z_1z_2%29%2C%20&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle 2 z_1 - 3 z_1^2 z_2 - z_1^3 z_3\Big)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%202%20z_1%20-%203%20z_1%5E2%20z_2%20-%20z_1%5E3%20z_3%5CBig%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and one can verify by a brief calculation that

![\displaystyle  \mathrm{det} DF = -2](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5Cmathrm%7Bdet%7D%20DF%20%3D%20-2&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and

![\displaystyle  F(0,0,-1/4) = F(1,-3/2, 13/2) = F(-1,3/2,13/2)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20F%280%2C0%2C-1%2F4%29%20%3D%20F%281%2C-3%2F2%2C%2013%2F2%29%20%3D%20F%28-1%2C3%2F2%2C13%2F2%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle  = (-1/4,0,0).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%3D%20%28-1%2F4%2C0%2C0%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

While this is an extremely quick verification, the construction presented in this fashion appears like a massive miracle. The polynomial ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) has degree seven, so *a priori* the Jacobian ![{\mathrm{det} DF}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7Bdet%7D%20DF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) ought to be a polynomial in three variables of degree as large as ![{3 \times 6 = 18}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B3%20%5Ctimes%206%20%3D%2018%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), so the fact that all non-constant coefficients of this polynomial vanish looks like a massive cancellation involving ![{\binom{18+3}{3}-1 = 1299}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbinom%7B18%2B3%7D%7B3%7D-1%20%3D%201299%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) equations, which is much larger than the ![{\binom{7+3}{3} = 120}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbinom%7B7%2B3%7D%7B3%7D%20%3D%20120%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) degrees of freedom for a generic degree seven polynomial of three variables. So finding such a polynomial looks highly unlikely to be located by brute force.

The example has since been retroactively [explained in more geometric terms](https://x.com/davikrehalt/status/2079175065695035442). As a “digestion” exercise to myself, I sought to write this explanation with relatively little use of algebraic geometry, in a manner that minimizes the amount of “miracles” required, although there are still a few places were some remarkable phenomena occur.

It is convenient to use the local injectivity formulation, and to generalize the domain ![{{\bf C}^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to an equivalent affine variety. Namely, we will show

> **Theorem 3 (Counterexample, reformulated)** []()There exists an affine variety ![{X \subset {\bf C}^5}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX%20%5Csubset%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E5%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) that is isomorphic to ![{{\bf C}^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by polynomial changes of variable, and a polynomial map ![{F : X \rightarrow {\bf C}^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%20%3A%20X%20%5Crightarrow%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) which is locally injective, but not globally injective.

Clearly one can get from Theorem [3](#jac2) to Theorem [2](#jac1) by composing with the isomorphism ![{X \cong {\bf C}^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX%20%5Ccong%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and using the previously mentioned fact that local injectivity implies non-zero constant Jacobian. Our objective is now to find data ![{X}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![{F : X \rightarrow {\bf C}^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%20%3A%20X%20%5Crightarrow%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) that obeys three separate properties:

The advantage of splitting the problem in to these three components is that we can build towards each of them separately.

It turns out that ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![{X}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can be built out of the operation of multiplication of low degree polynomials. Namely, consider the following three simple affine spaces:

(The notation ![{\mathrm{Sym}^k(V)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5Ek%28V%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) here refers to the ![{k^{th}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%5E%7Bth%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) [symmetric power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symmetric_power) of a vector space ![{V}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).) Clearly these spaces are isomorphic to ![{{\bf C}^2, {\bf C}^3, {\bf C}^4}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%2C%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%2C%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E4%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) respectively. Furthermore, we have a multiplication map ![{F : \mathrm{Sym}^1({\bf C}^2) \times \mathrm{Sym}^2({\bf C}^2) \rightarrow \mathrm{Sym}^3({\bf C}^2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%20%3A%20%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E1%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%20%5Ctimes%20%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E2%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%20%5Crightarrow%20%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E3%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), mapping a pair ![{(L,Q)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28L%2CQ%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of a linear polynomial ![{L}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and a quadratic polynomial ![{Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to a cubic polynomial

![\displaystyle F(L,Q) := LQ.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20F%28L%2CQ%29%20%3A%3D%20LQ.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

(Right now, the domain and range of this map ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is larger dimensional than the target of three; we will cut the dimensions down to three as the argument progresses.)

The map ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), essentially a map from ![{{\bf C}^5}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E5%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to ![{{\bf C}^4}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E4%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), is clearly polynomial; it is given explicitly in coordinates as[]()

[![\displaystyle  F( (a,b), (c,d,e) ) = (ac, ad + bc, ae + bd, be). \ \ \ \ \ (2)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20F%28%20%28a%2Cb%29%2C%20%28c%2Cd%2Ce%29%20%29%20%3D%20%28ac%2C%20ad%20%2B%20bc%2C%20ae%20%2B%20bd%2C%20be%29.%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%282%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

[]()The map ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) also enjoys two basic (and commuting) symmetries:

So this map enjoys a huge amount of equivariance, basically with respect to an action of the five-dimensional group ![{{\bf C}^\times \times {\bf C}^\times \times \mathrm{SL}_2({\bf C})}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E%5Ctimes%20%5Ctimes%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E%5Ctimes%20%5Ctimes%20%5Cmathrm%7BSL%7D_2%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

The five-dimensional domain ![{\mathrm{Sym}^1({\bf C}^2) \times \mathrm{Sym}^2({\bf C}^2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E1%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%20%5Ctimes%20%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E2%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is of course larger than the four-dimensional range ![{\mathrm{Sym}^3({\bf C}^2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E3%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), so the map ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) clearly cannot be injective. This can already be seen from the scaling symmetry, as the specific scalings[]()

[![\displaystyle  (L, Q) \mapsto (\lambda L, \lambda^{-1} Q) \ \ \ \ \ (3)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%28L%2C%20Q%29%20%5Cmapsto%20%28%5Clambda%20L%2C%20%5Clambda%5E%7B-1%7D%20Q%29%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%283%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

[]()for ![{\lambda \in {\bf C}^\times}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%20%5Cin%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E%5Ctimes%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) modify the linear and quadratic polynomials ![{L,Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%2CQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) but not their product ![{C = LQ}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BC%20%3D%20LQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). But even if one quotients out by this symmetry [(3)](#specific) to cut the dimension of the domain down to four, the map ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is still not injective for the following basic reason. A generically chosen cubic polynomial ![{C}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BC%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will split into the product ![{C = L_1 L_2 L_3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BC%20%3D%20L_1%20L_2%20L_3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of three independent linear polynomials. Then there are three pairs

[![\displaystyle  (L_1, L_2 L_3), (L_2, L_1 L_3), (L_3, L_1 L_2) \ \ \ \ \ (4)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%28L_1%2C%20L_2%20L_3%29%2C%20%28L_2%2C%20L_1%20L_3%29%2C%20%28L_3%2C%20L_1%20L_2%29%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%284%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

which all map to the same cubic polynomial

![\displaystyle  F(L_1, L_2 L_3) = F(L_2, L_1 L_3) = F(L_3, L_1 L_2) = C](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20F%28L_1%2C%20L_2%20L_3%29%20%3D%20F%28L_2%2C%20L_1%20L_3%29%20%3D%20F%28L_3%2C%20L_1%20L_2%29%20%3D%20C&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

under the multiplication map ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), but are not related to each other by scaling symmetry [(3)](#specific). Thus, we see that even after quotienting out by the scaling symmetry [(3)](#specific), the multiplication map ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is generically non-injective in a three-to-one fashion. Thus we already have achieved something resembling goal (b)!

It will be convenient to “spend” the scaling symmetry ![{(L, Q) \mapsto (\lambda L, \lambda^{-1} Q)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28L%2C%20Q%29%20%5Cmapsto%20%28%5Clambda%20L%2C%20%5Clambda%5E%7B-1%7D%20Q%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to obtain a useful normalization. If ![{L(z,w) = az+bw}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%28z%2Cw%29%20%3D%20az%2Bbw%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a linear polynomial and ![{Q(z,w)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%28z%2Cw%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a quadratic polynomial, the [resultant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resultant) ![{\mathrm{Res}(L,Q)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can be defined by the determinant[]()

[![\displaystyle  \mathrm{Res}(L,Q) = \begin{vmatrix} a & b & 0 \\ 0 & a & b \\ c & d & e \end{vmatrix} = a^2 e - abd + c b^2. \ \ \ \ \ (5)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29%20%3D%20%5Cbegin%7Bvmatrix%7D%20a%20%26%20b%20%26%200%20%5C%5C%200%20%26%20a%20%26%20b%20%5C%5C%20c%20%26%20d%20%26%20e%20%5Cend%7Bvmatrix%7D%20%3D%20a%5E2%20e%20-%20abd%20%2B%20c%20b%5E2.%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%285%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

[]()If we have a factoring

![\displaystyle  L(z,w) = a (z - \alpha w), \quad Q(z,w) = c (z - \beta_1 w)(z - \beta_2 w)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20L%28z%2Cw%29%20%3D%20a%20%28z%20-%20%5Calpha%20w%29%2C%20%5Cquad%20Q%28z%2Cw%29%20%3D%20c%20%28z%20-%20%5Cbeta_1%20w%29%28z%20-%20%5Cbeta_2%20w%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

then the resultant can also be described as

![\displaystyle  \mathrm{Res}(L,Q) = a^2 c (\alpha - \beta_1) (\alpha - \beta_2).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29%20%3D%20a%5E2%20c%20%28%5Calpha%20-%20%5Cbeta_1%29%20%28%5Calpha%20-%20%5Cbeta_2%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Thus the resultant measures whether the linear polynomial ![{L}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and the quadratic polynomial ![{Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) share a common root. A fundamental fact about resultants is that they are ![{SL_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BSL_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-invariant: for any ![{T \in SL_2({\bf C})}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BT%20%5Cin%20SL_2%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we have

![\displaystyle  \mathrm{Res}(L \circ T, Q \circ T) = \mathrm{Res}(L,Q).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%20%5Ccirc%20T%2C%20Q%20%5Ccirc%20T%29%20%3D%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

One way to see this is to check it first for translations ![{(z,w) \mapsto (z, w + h z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28z%2Cw%29%20%5Cmapsto%20%28z%2C%20w%20%2B%20h%20z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (which translate the roots ![{\alpha,\beta_1,\beta_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Calpha%2C%5Cbeta_1%2C%5Cbeta_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by ![{h}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bh%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) while leaving ![{a,c}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%2Cc%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) unchanged) and for inversions ![{(z,w) \mapsto (w,z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28z%2Cw%29%20%5Cmapsto%20%28w%2Cz%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (which map ![{\alpha,\beta_1,\beta_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Calpha%2C%5Cbeta_1%2C%5Cbeta_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to ![{1/\alpha, 1/\beta_1, 1/\beta_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1%2F%5Calpha%2C%201%2F%5Cbeta_1%2C%201%2F%5Cbeta_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) while mapping ![{a,c}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%2Cc%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to ![{-a\alpha}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B-a%5Calpha%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![{c\beta_1 \beta_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bc%5Cbeta_1%20%5Cbeta_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) respectively), and then noting that these transformations generate all of ![{SL_2({\bf C})}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BSL_2%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). They also interact very nicely with scaling:

![\displaystyle  \mathrm{Res}(\lambda_1 L, \lambda_2 Q) = \lambda_1^2 \lambda_2 \mathrm{Res}(L,Q).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28%5Clambda_1%20L%2C%20%5Clambda_2%20Q%29%20%3D%20%5Clambda_1%5E2%20%5Clambda_2%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

In particular, the scaling symmetry [(3)](#specific) multiplies ![{\mathrm{Res}(L,Q)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by ![{\lambda}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002):

[![\displaystyle  \mathrm{Res}(\lambda L, \lambda^{-1} Q) = \lambda \mathrm{Res}(L,Q). \ \ \ \ \ (6)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28%5Clambda%20L%2C%20%5Clambda%5E%7B-1%7D%20Q%29%20%3D%20%5Clambda%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29.%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%286%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

Thus, we can (generically) normalize away this scaling symmetry by imposing the condition

[![\displaystyle  \mathrm{Res}(L,Q) = 1. \ \ \ \ \ (7)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29%20%3D%201.%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%287%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

We now have a restricted multiplication map (which by abuse of notation we will continue to call ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)) from the four-dimensional variety[]()

[![\displaystyle  \{ (L,Q) \in \mathrm{Sym}^1({\bf C}^2) \times \mathrm{Sym}^2({\bf C}^2) : \mathrm{Res}(L,Q) = 1\} \ \ \ \ \ (8)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5C%7B%20%28L%2CQ%29%20%5Cin%20%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E1%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%20%5Ctimes%20%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E2%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%20%3A%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29%20%3D%201%5C%7D%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%288%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

[]()to the four-dimensional space ![{\mathrm{Sym}^3({\bf C}^2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E3%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This map ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is still not globally injective, as we can take the three pairs in [(4)](#l123) from before and apply the scaling [(3)](#specific) separately to each of the three pairs to obtain the normalization [(7)](#res1). So we have kept property (b). Furthermore, this map retains the ![{SL_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BSL_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-equivariance (and also one remaining scaling symmetry, though we will not make much further use of that symmetry).

But we now also have property (a)! Suppose we want to show the local injectivity of ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in the neighborhood of a pair ![{(L,Q)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28L%2CQ%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![{\mathrm{Res}(L,Q) = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29%20%3D%201%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). As the resultant is non-vanishing, the root ![{\alpha}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Calpha%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of ![{L}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (which exists in the Riemann sphere, or projective line if you prefer) is distinct from the two roots ![{\beta_1, \beta_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbeta_1%2C%20%5Cbeta_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of ![{Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (though the latter two roots could be equal to each other). Applying the ![{SL_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BSL_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) action (which performs Möbius transforms on the roots), one can assume without loss of generality that ![{\alpha}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Calpha%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the point at infinity (or equivalently ![{a=0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%3D0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)), thus ![{L(z,w) = b w}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%28z%2Cw%29%20%3D%20b%20w%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some complex number ![{b}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![{Q(z,w) = c (z - \beta_1 w)(z - \beta_2 w)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%28z%2Cw%29%20%3D%20c%20%28z%20-%20%5Cbeta_1%20w%29%28z%20-%20%5Cbeta_2%20w%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some complex numbers ![{c, \beta_1, \beta_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bc%2C%20%5Cbeta_1%2C%20%5Cbeta_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), with the resultant condition [(7)](#res1) simplifies to ![{cb^2 = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bcb%5E2%20%3D%201%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (so in particular ![{c,b}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bc%2Cb%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are also non-zero). It is then clear that if one perturbs ![{L}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![{Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by a small amount (say, modifying each coefficient by ![{O(\varepsilon)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BO%28%5Cvarepsilon%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)), then the root ![{\alpha=\infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Calpha%3D%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of ![{L}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will perturb to something large (![{\gg 1/\varepsilon}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cgg%201%2F%5Cvarepsilon%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)), while the roots ![{\beta_1,\beta_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbeta_1%2C%5Cbeta_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of ![{Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) stay bounded. Thus, just from knowledge of the product ![{F(L,Q)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%28L%2CQ%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), one can reconstruct which of the three roots of this cubic polynomial will be the perturbed root of ![{L}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and which two will be the perturbed roots of ![{Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); from this and [(6)](#scal), [(7)](#res1) we can also reconstruct the leading coefficient ![{c}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bc%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of ![{Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and this completely determines both ![{L}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![{Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This establishes the local injectivity property (a). (In fact it is [étale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formally_%C3%A9tale_morphism), but we will not need the machinery of étale maps here.)

Unfortunately, (the four-dimensional analogue of) condition (c) fails: the quadric hypersurface [(8)](#quad) is not isomorphic to the affine space ![{{\bf C}^4}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E4%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). But we can try to get around this by passing to a three-dimensional slice. Let ![{V}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be some three-dimensional affine plane of ![{\mathrm{Sym}^3({\bf C}^2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E3%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (which we will take to avoid the origin for technical reasons), then we can restrict ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as a map from the set[]()

[![\displaystyle  \{ (L,Q) \in \mathrm{Sym}^1({\bf C}^2) \times \mathrm{Sym}^2({\bf C}^2) : \mathrm{Res}(L,Q) = 1; \ \ \ \ \ (9)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5C%7B%20%28L%2CQ%29%20%5Cin%20%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E1%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%20%5Ctimes%20%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E2%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%20%3A%20%5Cmathrm%7BRes%7D%28L%2CQ%29%20%3D%201%3B%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%289%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

![\displaystyle  F(L,Q) \in V\}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20F%28L%2CQ%29%20%5Cin%20V%5C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

to ![{V}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). The latter is clearly identifiable (by linear changes of coordinate) to ![{{\bf C}^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). As ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) was already locally invertible, it remains locally invertible under restriction; and because generic cubic polynomials ![{C}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BC%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) had three preimages under ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in [(8)](#quad), this continues to be the case after restricting to [(9)](#quad-v) (unless ![{V}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) was somehow so degenerate that it had no generic elements, but this turns out to be impossible). So we have retained properties (a) and (b). The miracle is that, with a good choice of ![{V}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we can also obtain (c) and obtain the desired counterexample to the Jacobian conjecture: despite appearances, the variety [(9)](#quad-v) is in fact equivalent to the affine space ![{{\bf C}^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by polynomial changes of variable!

Let’s see how. The affine hyperplanes in ![{\mathrm{Sym}^3({\bf C}^2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E3%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) avoiding the origin are parameterized by the dual space of ![{\mathrm{Sym}^3({\bf C}^2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E3%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) avoiding the origin, which one can think of as the non-zero third order homogeneous differential operators ![{D = j \partial_z^3 + k \partial_z^2 \partial_w + l \partial_z \partial_w^2 + m \partial_w^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%20%3D%20j%20%5Cpartial_z%5E3%20%2B%20k%20%5Cpartial_z%5E2%20%5Cpartial_w%20%2B%20l%20%5Cpartial_z%20%5Cpartial_w%5E2%20%2B%20m%20%5Cpartial_w%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in two variables. Indeed, every such operator ![{D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) generates an affine hyperplane ![{\{ C \in \mathrm{Sym}^3({\bf C}^2) : D(C) = 1\}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5C%7B%20C%20%5Cin%20%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E3%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%20%3A%20D%28C%29%20%3D%201%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) that avoids the origin, and conversely by duality every affine hyperplane avoiding the origin arises in this form uniquely. Just as the cubic polynomials in ![{\mathrm{Sym}^3({\bf C}^2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E3%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can be factored into three linear polynomials, the differential operators in the dual space ![{\mathrm{Sym}^3({\bf C}^2)^*}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cmathrm%7BSym%7D%5E3%28%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%29%5E%2A%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can also be factored into three linear differential operators, e.g.,

![\displaystyle  D = j (\partial_z - \gamma_1 \partial_w) (\partial_z - \gamma_2 \partial_w) (\partial_z - \gamma_3 \partial_w)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20D%20%3D%20j%20%28%5Cpartial_z%20-%20%5Cgamma_1%20%5Cpartial_w%29%20%28%5Cpartial_z%20-%20%5Cgamma_2%20%5Cpartial_w%29%20%28%5Cpartial_z%20-%20%5Cgamma_3%20%5Cpartial_w%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

in the case that ![{j}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bj%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is non-zero. The ![{SL_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BSL_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) action moves the roots ![{\gamma_1,\gamma_2,\gamma_3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cgamma_1%2C%5Cgamma_2%2C%5Cgamma_3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) around the Riemann sphere by Möbius transformations. As these transformations are ![{3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-transitive, the actual selection of such roots is not too important (and the scaling symmetry similarly makes the choice of leading coefficient ![{j}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bj%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) unimportant); the only thing to keep track of is whether the roots repeat. Up to the symmetries, there are in fact just three different equivalence classes of differential operator ![{D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (and thus of affine hyperplane ![{V}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)) to consider:

It turns out that the affine miracle for [(9)](#quad-v) occurs precisely in the second case, when ![{D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) has two identical roots. I do not have a completely satisfactory geometric explanation for this miracle, but one can verify it by the following coordinate computation.

By applying the ![{SL_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BSL_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) action, we can normalize so that ![{D = \partial_z^2 \partial_w}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%20%3D%20%5Cpartial_z%5E2%20%5Cpartial_w%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), thus ![{V}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is now the affine hyperplane of cubic polynomials ![{C(z,w) = f z^3 + g z^2 w + h z w^2 + i w^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BC%28z%2Cw%29%20%3D%20f%20z%5E3%20%2B%20g%20z%5E2%20w%20%2B%20h%20z%20w%5E2%20%2B%20i%20w%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![{g=1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%3D1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Using [(2)](#mult) and [(5)](#reslq), the variety [(9)](#quad-v) can now be described explicitly in coordinates as[]()

[![\displaystyle  \{ (a,b,c,d,e) \in {\bf C}^5 : a^2 e - abd + cb^2 = 1; ad + bc = 1 \}. \ \ \ \ \ (10)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5C%7B%20%28a%2Cb%2Cc%2Cd%2Ce%29%20%5Cin%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E5%20%3A%20a%5E2%20e%20-%20abd%20%2B%20cb%5E2%20%3D%201%3B%20ad%20%2B%20bc%20%3D%201%20%5C%7D.%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%2810%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

[]()At first glance this seems to be a generic-looking variety cut out by a cubic equation and a quadratic equation – hardly a candidate to be affine! But observe that if ![{a}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is non-zero, then the second equation ![{ad+bc = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bad%2Bbc%20%3D%201%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can be solved for ![{d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002),

[![\displaystyle  d = \frac{1 - bc}{a} \ \ \ \ \ (11)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20d%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1%20-%20bc%7D%7Ba%7D%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%2811%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

and the first equation ![{a^2 e - abd + cb^2 = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%5E2%20e%20-%20abd%20%2B%20cb%5E2%20%3D%201%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can be solved for ![{e}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Be%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002),

[![\displaystyle  e = \frac{1 + abd - cb^2}{a^2}. \ \ \ \ \ (12)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20e%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1%20%2B%20abd%20-%20cb%5E2%7D%7Ba%5E2%7D.%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%2812%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

Putting these two equations together, we see that as long as one removes the case ![{a=0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%3D0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), the quintuple ![{(a,b,c,d,e)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a%2Cb%2Cc%2Cd%2Ce%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is uniquely determined by ![{(a,b,c)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a%2Cb%2Cc%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by a change of variables which is Laurent in ![{a}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and polynomial in ![{b,c}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%2Cc%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Thus we have a nice birational equivalence

![\displaystyle  \{ (a,b,c,d,e) \in {\bf C}^5 : a^2 e - abd + cb^2 = 1; ad + bc = 1; a \neq 0 \}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5C%7B%20%28a%2Cb%2Cc%2Cd%2Ce%29%20%5Cin%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E5%20%3A%20a%5E2%20e%20-%20abd%20%2B%20cb%5E2%20%3D%201%3B%20ad%20%2B%20bc%20%3D%201%3B%20a%20%5Cneq%200%20%5C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle  \cong \{ (a,b,c) \in {\bf C}^3 : a \neq 0 \}.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5Ccong%20%5C%7B%20%28a%2Cb%2Cc%29%20%5Cin%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%20%3A%20a%20%5Cneq%200%20%5C%7D.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Thus we have already *almost* established property (c): the variety [(9)](#quad-v) becomes birationally equivalent to ![{{\bf C}^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) after cutting out the ![{a=0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%3D0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) subvariety. In particular, for each fixed non-zero value ![{a_0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of ![{a}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), the corresponding fiber

![\displaystyle  \{ (a,b,c,d,e) \in {\bf C}^5 : a^2 e - abd + cb^2 = 1; ad + bc = 1; a = a_0 \}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5C%7B%20%28a%2Cb%2Cc%2Cd%2Ce%29%20%5Cin%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E5%20%3A%20a%5E2%20e%20-%20abd%20%2B%20cb%5E2%20%3D%201%3B%20ad%20%2B%20bc%20%3D%201%3B%20a%20%3D%20a_0%20%5C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

of [(10)](#explicit) is equivalent to ![{{\bf C}^2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by polynomial changes of variable, since we can reconstruct ![{d,e}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%2Ce%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) from the coordinates ![{b,c}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%2Cc%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by the polynomial formulae

![\displaystyle  d = \frac{1-bc}{a_0}; \quad e = \frac{1 + a_0 d b - c b^2}{a_0^2}.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20d%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1-bc%7D%7Ba_0%7D%3B%20%5Cquad%20e%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1%20%2B%20a_0%20d%20b%20-%20c%20b%5E2%7D%7Ba_0%5E2%7D.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

So we just need to glue back in the ![{a=0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%3D0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) fiber. Indeed, from [(10)](#explicit) we see that the fiber at ![{0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is just

![\displaystyle  \{ (0,b,c,d,e) \in {\bf C}^5 : cb^2 = 1; bc = 1 \}. ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5C%7B%20%280%2Cb%2Cc%2Cd%2Ce%29%20%5Cin%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E5%20%3A%20cb%5E2%20%3D%201%3B%20bc%20%3D%201%20%5C%7D.%20&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Now we observe a key miracle: the cubic equation ![{cb^2 = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bcb%5E2%20%3D%201%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and quadratic equation ![{bc = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bbc%20%3D%201%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) have a unique affine solution ![{b=c=1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%3Dc%3D1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (as opposed to the six possible solutions that Bezout’s theorem might suggest – the other five solutions live on the line at infinity). So the fiber here is also affine:

![\displaystyle  \{ (0,1,1,d,e) \in {\bf C}^5 : d, e \in {\bf C} \}. ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5C%7B%20%280%2C1%2C1%2Cd%2Ce%29%20%5Cin%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E5%20%3A%20d%2C%20e%20%5Cin%20%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%20%5C%7D.%20&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

This is extremely encouraging for the purposes of establishing property (c), as it strongly suggests that the variety [(10)](#explicit) has the structure of an ![{{\bf C}^2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-bundle over ![{{\bf C}^1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), which is already extremely close to being isomorphic to the affine space ![{{\bf C}^3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf%20C%7D%5E3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). The main remaining task is to make sure that nothing singular happens in the limit ![{a \rightarrow 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%20%5Crightarrow%200%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and that a global polynomial coordinate chart for [(10)](#explicit) that covers both the ![{a \neq 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%20%5Cneq%200%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![{a = 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%20%3D%200%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) fibers can be constructed.

The standard way to proceed here is to manipulate various tangent spaces using the modern machinery of algebraic geometry and commutative algebra, but given my own background, I prefer to adopt the language of analysis, and in particular big-O notation (in place of the ideals used in algebraic geometry), in order to investigate the limit ![{a \rightarrow 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%20%5Crightarrow%200%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by hand. On the variety [(10)](#explicit), let us use ![{O(X)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BO%28X%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to denote any multiple of ![{X}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by a polynomial expression in ![{a,b,c,d,e}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%2Cb%2Cc%2Cd%2Ce%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Thus, for instance, the equation ![{ad + bc = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bad%20%2B%20bc%20%3D%201%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) implies that[]()

[![\displaystyle  bc = 1 + O(a) \ \ \ \ \ (13)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20bc%20%3D%201%20%2B%20O%28a%29%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%2813%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

[]()while the equation ![{a^2 e - abd + cb^2 = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%5E2%20e%20-%20abd%20%2B%20cb%5E2%20%3D%201%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) implies that

[![\displaystyle  cb^2 = 1 + O(a) \ \ \ \ \ (14)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20cb%5E2%20%3D%201%20%2B%20O%28a%29%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%2814%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

as well as the more refined estimate

[![\displaystyle  cb^2 = 1 + abd + O(a^2). \ \ \ \ \ (15)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20cb%5E2%20%3D%201%20%2B%20abd%20%2B%20O%28a%5E2%29.%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%2815%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

In the ![{a=0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%3D0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) case we could conclude that ![{b=c=1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%3Dc%3D1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Now we perturb this observation. Multiplying [(13)](#bc-1) by ![{b}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) we have ![{b^2 c = b + O(a)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%5E2%20c%20%3D%20b%20%2B%20O%28a%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), which on substitution into [(14)](#bc-2) gives ![{b = 1 + O(a)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%20%3D%201%20%2B%20O%28a%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); substituting this back into either [(13)](#bc-1) or [(14)](#bc-2) also gives ![{c = 1 + O(a)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bc%20%3D%201%20%2B%20O%28a%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

We can get some more precise asymptotics by also taking advantage of [(15)](#bc-3). Substituting ![{ad+bc=1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bad%2Bbc%3D1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) into [(15)](#bc-3), we obtain after some algebra

![\displaystyle  2 cb^2 = 1 + b + O(a^2).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%202%20cb%5E2%20%3D%201%20%2B%20b%20%2B%20O%28a%5E2%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

So if we write ![{b = 1+O(a)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%20%3D%201%2BO%28a%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) more explicitly as ![{b = 1 + a y}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%20%3D%201%20%2B%20a%20y%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then we have

![\displaystyle  2 c (1 + 2ay + O(a^2)) = 2 + ay + O(a^2)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%202%20c%20%281%20%2B%202ay%20%2B%20O%28a%5E2%29%29%20%3D%202%20%2B%20ay%20%2B%20O%28a%5E2%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and thus

[![\displaystyle  c = 1 - \frac{3}{2} ay + O(a^2). \ \ \ \ \ (16)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20c%20%3D%201%20-%20%5Cfrac%7B3%7D%7B2%7D%20ay%20%2B%20O%28a%5E2%29.%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%5C%20%2816%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)]()

Substituting this back into [(11)](#d-eq) gives an asymptotic for ![{d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002):

![\displaystyle  d = \frac{1 - bc}{a} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20d%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1%20-%20bc%7D%7Ba%7D%20&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle = \frac{1 - (1 + ay) (1 - \frac{3}{2} ay + O(a^2))}{a}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1%20-%20%281%20%2B%20ay%29%20%281%20-%20%5Cfrac%7B3%7D%7B2%7D%20ay%20%2B%20O%28a%5E2%29%29%7D%7Ba%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle  =\frac{1}{2} y + O(a).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%3D%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B2%7D%20y%20%2B%20O%28a%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Finally, one can insert these estimates into [(12)](#e-eq), although one only gets a trivial bound in this case:

![\displaystyle  e = \frac{1 + abd - cb^2}{a^2} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20e%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1%20%2B%20abd%20-%20cb%5E2%7D%7Ba%5E2%7D%20&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle = \frac{1 + a (1+O(a)) (\frac{1}{2} y + O(a)) - (1 - \frac{3}{2} ay + O(a^2)) (1 + ay)^2}{a^2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1%20%2B%20a%20%281%2BO%28a%29%29%20%28%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B2%7D%20y%20%2B%20O%28a%29%29%20-%20%281%20-%20%5Cfrac%7B3%7D%7B2%7D%20ay%20%2B%20O%28a%5E2%29%29%20%281%20%2B%20ay%29%5E2%7D%7Ba%5E2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle  = O(1).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%3D%20O%281%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Expanding the ![{O(a^2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BO%28a%5E2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) error term in [(16)](#c-eq) as ![{a^2 z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%5E2%20z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and doing a little more algebra, we thus have a polynomial change of variables

![\displaystyle  a = a](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20a%20%3D%20a&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle  b = 1 + ay](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20b%20%3D%201%20%2B%20ay&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle  c = 1 - \frac{3}{2} ay + a^2 z](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20c%20%3D%201%20-%20%5Cfrac%7B3%7D%7B2%7D%20ay%20%2B%20a%5E2%20z&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle  d = \frac{1-bc}{a} = \frac{1}{2} y - az + \frac{3}{2} a y^2 - a^2 yz](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20d%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1-bc%7D%7Ba%7D%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B2%7D%20y%20-%20az%20%2B%20%5Cfrac%7B3%7D%7B2%7D%20a%20y%5E2%20-%20a%5E2%20yz&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle  e = \frac{1 + abd - cb^2}{a^2} = -2z + 4y^2 - 4ayz + 3ay^3 - 2a^2 y^2 z](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20e%20%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1%20%2B%20abd%20-%20cb%5E2%7D%7Ba%5E2%7D%20%3D%20-2z%20%2B%204y%5E2%20-%204ayz%20%2B%203ay%5E3%20-%202a%5E2%20y%5E2%20z&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

which completely parameterizes the variety [(10)](#explicit) by polynomial combinations of three coordinates ![{a,y,z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%2Cy%2Cz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This already gives (a) and thus completes the proof of Theorem [3](#jac2).

The previous computations, when expanded out, also gives polynomial inverse maps:

![\displaystyle  a = a](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20a%20%3D%20a&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle  y = 2bd - ae](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20y%20%3D%202bd%20-%20ae&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![\displaystyle  z = 2d^2 + ce + 6bd^2 + 3bce - \frac{9}{2} e](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20z%20%3D%202d%5E2%20%2B%20ce%20%2B%206bd%5E2%20%2B%203bce%20-%20%5Cfrac%7B9%7D%7B2%7D%20e&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

The map from ![{(a,y,z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to the ![{(f,h,i)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28f%2Ch%2Ci%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) coefficients of ![{F(L,Q)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%28L%2CQ%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (dropping the ![{g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) coefficient which is constrained to equal ![{1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)), we obtain a polynomial map

![\displaystyle  (a,y,z) \mapsto (G_1(a,y,z), G_2(a,y,z), G_3(a,y,z))](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%20%5Cmapsto%20%28G_1%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%2C%20G_2%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%2C%20G_3%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

with

![\displaystyle  \begin{array}{rl}  G_1(a,y,z) &= a - \frac{3}{2} a^2 y + a^3 z \\ G_2(a,y,z) &= \frac{1}{2} y - 3az + 6ay^2 - 6a^2 yz + \frac{9}{2} a^2 y^3 - 3a^3 y^2 z \\ G_3(a,y,z) &= -2z + 4y^2 - 6ayz + 7ay^3 - 6a^2 y^2 z + 3a^2 y^4 \\ & \quad - 2 a^3 y^3 z \end{array} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%5Cbegin%7Barray%7D%7Brl%7D%20%20G_1%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%20%26%3D%20a%20-%20%5Cfrac%7B3%7D%7B2%7D%20a%5E2%20y%20%2B%20a%5E3%20z%20%5C%5C%20G_2%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%20%26%3D%20%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B2%7D%20y%20-%203az%20%2B%206ay%5E2%20-%206a%5E2%20yz%20%2B%20%5Cfrac%7B9%7D%7B2%7D%20a%5E2%20y%5E3%20-%203a%5E3%20y%5E2%20z%20%5C%5C%20G_3%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%20%26%3D%20-2z%20%2B%204y%5E2%20-%206ayz%20%2B%207ay%5E3%20-%206a%5E2%20y%5E2%20z%20%2B%203a%5E2%20y%5E4%20%5C%5C%20%26%20%5Cquad%20-%202%20a%5E3%20y%5E3%20z%20%5Cend%7Barray%7D%20&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

which theory predicts to have a constant Jacobian, and indeed one can calculate that the Jacobian is ![{-1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B-1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This is essentially the original example up to trivial changes of variable; indeed, one can check that the map

![\displaystyle  (a, y, -2z) \mapsto (G_3(a,y,z), 2G_2(a,y,z), 2G_1(a,y,z))](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle%20%20%28a%2C%20y%2C%20-2z%29%20%5Cmapsto%20%28G_3%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%2C%202G_2%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%2C%202G_1%28a%2Cy%2Cz%29%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

is exactly the map ![{F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) given in [(1)](#explicit-ex).

AI disclosure: I [used an AI chatbot](https://chatgpt.com/share/6a5fdc7a-d6f8-83e8-bbea-8deb42cfed56) to discuss various aspects of this problem and to confirm several of the calculations made here.

**[Legal](http://hintjens.com/legal:start) | [About](http://hintjens.com/about) | [Contact](http://hintjens.com/contact)**

Ten Steps Towards Happiness

[![pieterh](http://www.wikidot.com/avatar.php?userid=99&amp%3Bsize=small&amp%3Btimestamp=1473156446)](http://www.wikidot.com/user:info/pieterh)[pieterh](http://www.wikidot.com/user:info/pieterh) wrote on 29 Sep 2015 09:57

[![battery_full.png](http://hintjens.wdfiles.com/local--resized-images/blog:99/battery_full.png/small.jpg)](http://hintjens.wdfiles.com/local--files/blog:99/battery_full.png)

Today, I'm going to explain ten simple steps you can take towards being happier, and making those around you happier. It takes no money and no magic. Just a shift in how you see the world, yourself, and others. As the 14th Dalai Lama said, "Happiness… comes from your own actions."

1\. Invest in your senses

Your body and mind need a good diet. Make your own food, and make it tasty. Take photographs you find beautiful. Play music that excites you. Wear clothes that feel nice. Walk through the city at night, in the rain. Feel the texture of your life, with all your senses.

2\. Do things you are bad at

Learning makes us feel alive. Challenge yourself, and keep proving you can learn. Learn to juggle, to hold your breath underwater for longer, to solve a Rubik's cube. Learn to play music and play for yourself. Learn to paint and draw.

3\. Enjoy other people

Nothing makes us happier than other people. Take the time to talk to people you cross paths with. Be nice, chat, smile. If you live far from other people, move to be closer. If you drive a lot, change your life so you can walk and cycle. Go out and mingle.

4\. Be part of bigger things

Being part of bigger things makes us feel valued. Find communities and projects to be part of. Start your own. It could be online or in the real world. Even the smallest contributions can make a difference to someone. Be precious to strangers.

5\. Finish your projects

A sense of achievement gives us hope and confidence. Write down your life's accomplishments. Even small ones. Now keep this list up to date. Every year, think of what you've achieved. Finish your projects and move on to new ones.

6\. Get rid of bad actors

Nothing can make us more miserable than other people. Read ["The Psychopath Code,"](http://hintjens.com/blog:_psychopaths) and learn to recognize bad actors. When someone is a source of misery, remove them from your life. Whether it takes a day or a year, make this a project, and finish it.

7\. Ground your emotions

To be happy you must deal with negative emotions. Learn to recognize these in yourself, and [deal with them](http://hintjens.com/blog:81#toc12). Anger, self-pity, jealousy, fear, hate, loneliness… set them aside, and let happiness take their place. If you drink to relax, cut it out.

8\. Revalue your time

Stop wasting your time on commuting, boring jobs, meetings, TV. Do only things that you feel are worthwhile, with people you like. If this means a cut in income, so be it. Be the person you really want to be. Don't take it all too seriously, we all die.

9\. Travel light

Material possessions are more often a burden than a pleasure. Give away or sell anything you own that does not make you happy. Do not let your possessions define you. We live in a world of plenty. That means you can own less, not more.

10\. Want nothing, accept everything

Above all, explore the world without desire or demand, and be tolerant of whatever happens. Most people are nice, and even the others teach us. When you want nothing, you cannot be disappointed. When you accept everything, you will see beauty in every moment.

## [HN-TITLE] 17. Long presumed dead, a thriving coral reef is discovered in West Africa

- **Source**: [https://e360.yale.edu/digest/benin-coral-reef](https://e360.yale.edu/digest/benin-coral-reef)
- **Site**: Yale E360
- **Author**: By Richard Schiffman
- **Submitted**: 2026-07-21 15:41 UTC (Hacker News)
- **HN activity**: 364 points · [79 comments](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48993816)
- **Length**: 1.2K words (~6 min read)
- **Language**: en-US

This [article](https://insideclimatenews.org/news/20072026/west-africa-thriving-coral-reef/) was originally published by [Inside Climate News](https://insideclimatenews.org/) and is reproduced here as part of the [Climate Desk](https://www.climatedesk.org/) collaboration.

[![Soft corals discovered off the coast of Benin.](https://yale-threesixty.transforms.svdcdn.com/production/Benin-Coral_IRHOB.jpg?w=400&auto=compress%2Cformat&fit=clip&dm=1784630734&s=8cff546fb29e0a5cd5b910f4d1936787)](https://yale-threesixty.transforms.svdcdn.com/production/Benin-Coral_IRHOB.jpg?w=1200&h=800&auto=compress%2Cformat&fit=crop&dm=1784630734&s=26b17bad3d47252094454e5b6d7c98d0)

Soft corals discovered off the coast of Benin. Gérard Zinzindohoué / IRHOB

> Sixty years after surveyors first uncovered evidence of a sprawling coral reef off the West African nation of Benin, scientists have at last found the reef.

* * *

In the 1960s, fishing surveyors off West Africa’s Benin coast hauled up heads of coral in their nets. Employed by local governments to assess fish diversity and discover potentially trawlable seabeds in the sandy-bottomed Gulf of Guinea, the researchers shrugged off the discovery at the time, burying the find in a brief paragraph in a 130-something-page report.

With no surveys since, the scientists who followed had lost track of exactly where the reefs might lie. And as mass bleaching events and overfishing slashed the world’s coral reef area by more than [50 percent](https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/news/2021/september/over-half-of-coral-reef-cover-lost-since-1950.html) since the 1950s, local oceanographers had written off any remnants of a possible reef as dead.

More than six decades on, the mystery has been solved as a team of Beninese scientists [rediscovered](https://www.frontiersin.org/news/2026/07/20/underwater-drones-coral-reefs-benin-presumed-dead-teeming-life-frontiers-marine-science) the healthy reef teeming with marine life. At least eight coral types and eight fish species have formed a thriving ecosystem on this long-forgotten site. 

“I was guided by hope. A hope that sometimes there are species or ecosystems which kind of defeat time, or are trying to resist it,” said Gérard Zinzindohoué, the project lead for Coral Reefs Rediscovering & Exploration in Benin.

Fresh off seeing coral for the first time in Cape Verde in 2021, Zinzindohoué asked himself a simple question: Do we have any reefs in Benin?

After a colleague passed on the original 1960s report — which mapped out a potential 24-mile-long coral reef barrier along Benin’s coastline — Zinzindohoué recognized a gap in the scientific knowledge: “I realized how little we know about our own coastal environment.” 

But in the years that followed, a string of failed grant applications halted progress on solving the mystery.

When he secured a $20,000 National Geographic Explorers grant in January 2025, the project finally ignited. Yet getting the gear was also challenging. The sonar equipment swallowed up almost 80 percent of the grant budget and nearly stalled entirely when the European supplier refused to accept payment from Zinzindohoué’s African bank account.

To complicate matters further, Benin doesn’t have a permanent research vessel. Zinzindohoué therefore had no choice but to seek the help of local fishermen to ferry the team to the suspected site 14 miles offshore and jury-rig the pirogue to accommodate towing the high-tech sonar device.

“The fishermen’s boats are not used to going that far offshore, so it was risky for everyone,” said Zinzindohoué, whose team battled repeated engine failures and months of nausea-filled fieldwork before finally locating two strong sonar echoes pinging from the depths below. National Geographic — whom Zinzindohoué credits with the success of the project — then dispatched a high-resolution deep-sea camera system from their Exploration Technology Lab to investigate the sonar results.

[![Footage of the newly discovered coral reef.](https://yale-threesixty.transforms.svdcdn.com/production/Benin-Coral-GIF_IRHOB.gif?w=400&auto=compress%2Cformat&fit=clip&dm=1784630954&s=c7bd8ade901224b2c833a1fa82c05c53)](https://yale-threesixty.transforms.svdcdn.com/production/Benin-Coral-GIF_IRHOB.gif?w=800&h=450&auto=compress%2Cformat&fit=crop&dm=1784630954&s=f94074e87ac55a7357699451ed9cd93e)

Footage of the newly discovered coral reef. Gérard Zinzindohoué / IRHOB

While their wooden pirogue bounced atop the offshore swell, the team filmed the seafloor below but had to wait until they returned to dry land to scour the footage. What they had captured was a wealth of marine life: six types of soft coral; two black corals; and eight species of sheltering fish, from golden African snappers to the Monrovia doctorfish.

When the first images came through, Zinzindohoué texted Houangninan Midinoudewa, an oceanographic researcher at the Benin Marine Conservation Club and the same friend who first told Zinzindohoué of the 1960s report.

Midinoudewa remembers the moment: “I said, ‘Bro, 60 years later we are now discovering exactly what we have in our water.’ We found it still alive, producing more fish for communities and supporting livelihoods. I was really joyful and really overwhelmed.”

While no coral samples have yet been extracted, researchers have classified the site as a mesophotic coral ecosystem (MCE). Such systems are light-dependent communities existing at the lower limits of reef-building corals. At more than 175 feet below the surface, the coral garden they discovered appears patchily scattered on a rocky substrate.

Bridging the gap between shallow reefs and deeper benthic habitats, MCEs are home to distinct species and unique environmental conditions. While scientists are increasingly recognizing their ecological and climatic importance, they remain among the least explored elements of tropical and subtropical marine biodiversity. And this one has real potential to unlock new information about coral history.

“Since it’s an undisturbed marine ecosystem, it can help through carbon dating or paleoclimatic study to tell us which kind of climate system has occurred here in the past,” said Zinzindohoué. “It’s better to know the past to help explain the present, and help know which kind of direction we can take in the future.”

In addition to the blackbar soldierfish, West African goatfish, and Guinean angelfish filmed darting through the reef, the discovery presents potential conservation claims for other marine life.

“We will be advocating for its full protection, perhaps by setting up a marine protected area around it,” said Midinoudewa, who specializes in elasmobranchs — the study of sharks, rays, and skates.

Midinoudewa is currently submitting an application to designate the area as an Important Shark and Ray Area with the International Union for Conservation of Nature. Based on the knowledge of local fishermen, Midinoudewa is confident the reef is home to sawback angel sharks, silky sharks, brown skates, and marbled stingrays. 

The team behind the discovery hopes this will spark a wave of similar discoveries in the waters off West Africa, an area of the world underserved by scientific research projects 

“I hope the Gulf of Guinea will be a hub for research because we know our resources are being exploited and people \[need to] know exactly what we have and why we should care,” said Midinoudewa. 

Zinzindohoué agrees: “We don’t need to wait for others to come to our country to show us what is under our sea. We are the ones who must take responsibility.”

—*Johnny Sturgeon*, Inside Climate News

- **Submitted**: 2026-07-17 11:00 UTC (Hacker News)
- **HN activity**: 49 points · [22 comments](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48945804)
- **Length**: 538 words (~3 min read)
- **Language**: en

ScreenWall Beta

## Turn old phones into synced widgets for your space.

ScreenWall is browser-based software for old phones, tablets, monitors, and spare displays. Use it to build clocks, status boards, ambient screens, signage, and shared walls from the hardware you already have, without asking people to install anything first.

See a real setup

**Real smartphones, browser pairing, and synced screens in one controller.**This is a raw walkthrough of ScreenWall while pairing reused phones, arranging the layout, and driving a live screen setup.

Your browser cannot play the demo video. You can still open it directly at [/landing/screenwall-demo.mp4?v=20260712b](https://screenwall.app/landing/screenwall-demo.mp4?v=20260712b).

More real setups

**Small screens, real desks, no showroom mockups.**A few examples of ScreenWall running on ordinary devices in everyday spaces, exactly the kind of hardware this project is meant to reuse.

**Reuse what you own**Start with a few spare phones or tablets and grow only when the setup proves itself.

**No app install**Open a link, pair the screen, and manage everything from one controller.

**From widget to wall**Run a tiny status display, a row of old phones, or a full synced installation in the same system.

**Pair spare screens fast**Open a QR or PIN link on any browser and the device is ready to join almost immediately.

**Run synced widgets and dashboards**Show clocks, counters, arrivals, weather, status boards, or ambient visuals across multiple screens.

**Mix old and new devices**Use whatever survived the drawer, the office, or the last hardware refresh instead of waiting for a perfect match.

**Expand into bigger moments**Move from simple widgets to signage, ambient loops, and full multi-screen scenes without rebuilding the setup.

1

**Gather the screens you already have**

Bring spare phones, tablets, or monitors together and let each one join from its browser.

2

**Turn them into the display your space needs**

Arrange widgets, signage, ambient content, or a stretched scene across the full setup.

3

**Refresh the setup whenever the room changes**

Move from status wall to event backdrop to quiet ambient loop without touching every screen by hand.

Questions before you sign up?

## Common things people want to know first.

Do I need to install an app on every screen?

No. Screens join from the browser with a QR code or PIN, which makes it much easier to reuse spare phones and tablets.

Will this work with old or mixed devices?

That is one of the main goals. ScreenWall is built for mixed walls of phones, tablets, and monitors, including older devices and legacy browsers where possible.

Can I try it without a big setup?

Yes. You can start with a few devices, create a small wall, and see whether the experience fits your space before investing more time.

What can I put on a wall?

You can place images, videos, GIFs, slideshows, live canvas feeds, and programmable canvas apps for widgets or art-driven visuals.

Do I need perfect matching hardware?

No. The product is meant for real-world leftovers: different screen sizes, bezels, orientations, and device generations.

Is ScreenWall already finished?

Not yet. ScreenWall is still in beta, which means the core experience is usable, but we are still improving stability, compatibility, and polish.

Ready to try it?

**Create your first synced screen setup when you are ready.**You can look through the demo first, then sign up when the idea fits your devices and your space.

## [HN-TITLE] 19. "Drawing" the Mona Lisa with GPT-5.6, Claude, Gemini, and Grok

- **Source**: [https://www.tryai.dev/blog/ai-drawing-arena-colored-pencils-claude-gpt-grok](https://www.tryai.dev/blog/ai-drawing-arena-colored-pencils-claude-gpt-grok)
- **Site**: TryAI
- **Author**: TryAI
- **Published**: 2026-07-21
- **HN activity**: 219 points · [77 comments](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48998404)
- **Length**: 1.7K words (~8 min read)
- **Language**: en

We built a drawing arena: hand a model a blank white canvas and a set of colored-pencil tools, then get out of the way. The model sets a color, tip width, and pressure, lays down batches of strokes, smudges to blend, erases, and calls view\_canvas to see its own work and decide what to fix. It either reproduces a target image or draws from a text prompt.

We ran four vision models, [GPT-5.6 Sol](https://www.tryai.dev/models/gpt-5.6-sol), [Claude Fable 5](https://www.tryai.dev/models/claude-fable-5), [Grok 4.5](https://www.tryai.dev/models/grok-4.5), and [Gemini 3.6 Flash](https://www.tryai.dev/models/gemini-3.6-flash), across two targets (the Mona Lisa and Van Gogh's Starry Night, both scored objectively) and five open-ended prompts, for 28 drawings total. We'll cover tool use, cost, output, and whether the models actually improved their work, with our opinion at the end.

## Why we run these

A quick note on why we're doing this, because our [last one of these](https://www.tryai.dev/blog/ai-music-video-arena-claude-vs-gpt-5.6) (models making music videos) kicked off a good [discussion](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48939524) where some folks read it as us promoting the creativity or artistic ability of models. These are not objective tests of model capability. They are deliberately open-ended, fuzzy tasks. Here is why we personally keep running them:

- **They are fun, and genuinely informative.** Watching a model tackle a loose, open-ended task is a more interesting visual indicator of capability than yet another benchmark number.
- **They expose the real frontier-vs-open gap.** Cheaper open-weight models can absolutely replace the frontier for a lot of execution work, and we will keep reporting on that. But there is also a lot of benchmaxxing out there, and tasks like this cut through it. They genuinely separate frontier capability from the rest. Grok 4.5, as you will see, was rough at this "basic" drawing task, and the open-weight models we tried were not even usable, several just returned a blank canvas. (That may change with Kimi K3 which we will report on once it is fully open-sourced)
- **They show what long-running tasks actually cost.** Claude Fable 5 almost always took much longer than the others, for far more money, and here produced worse output. Fable has beaten GPT-5.6 for us on plenty of tasks, including the [music-video challenge](https://www.tryai.dev/blog/ai-music-video-arena-claude-vs-gpt-5.6), but on this one it was worse for a lot more overhead. Depending on your use case, that trade-off matters.
- **We're loving the discussions.** The discussions have genuinely been interesting, and if you have suggestions for the setup or new tasks, we will take them into account. These have been a blast to run, and the outputs are super exciting to see.

## The tools

Every model worked with the exact same colored-pencil toolset:

- **plan**: a no-op scratchpad for thinking and planning between steps.
- **view\_target**: look at the target image again.
- **view\_canvas**: render the current page and see it. This is also when we score the drawing against the target.
- **set\_color** / **set\_brush** / **set\_pressure**: the current pencil color, tip width, and pressure (0 to 1 opacity, low pressure for softer tone).
- **draw**: lay down a batch of marks (strokes, lines, shape outlines, dots) in one call. There are no solid fills, tone and color are built by layering, like a real pencil.
- **smudge**: blend/soften a rectangular region, like a blending stump.
- **erase** / **clear\_canvas**: lift a region back to white / reset the whole page.

The whole harness is open source at [github.com/hershalb/canvas-arena](https://github.com/hershalb/canvas-arena). Point it at any target image or a text prompt and run it yourself.

## The two target reproductions

The models could see the target the whole time and were scored on structural similarity (SSIM) to it. We show the SSIM scores below.

Mona Lisa (target)GPT-5.6 SolClaude Fable 5Grok 4.5Gemini 3.6 Flash

## The headline numbers

Averages and totals across all seven drawings per model.

ModelGPT-5.6 Sol7296.2 min3.4M$7.746.0116Claude Fable 575412.5 min14.6M$160.5815.6207Grok 4.57994.8 min34.0M$9.214.3354Gemini 3.6 Flash7736.9 min27.7M$12.8722.6190

## Tool calling

The four models used the exact same toolset in completely different ways.

GPT-5.6 SolClaude Fable 5

Grok 4.5Gemini 3.6 Flash

**GPT-5.6 Sol never called set\_color, set\_brush, or set\_pressure once**, it set those inline on each draw call instead, so its calls are almost all draw, smudge, and view\_canvas. **Grok 4.5 did the opposite**: 65% of its 1,349 tool calls were set\_color / set\_brush / set\_pressure, which is why it averaged 99 steps per drawing. **Claude Fable 5** leaned on smudge (123 calls) and reviewed constantly. **Gemini 3.6 Flash** was the most obsessive reviewer of all, nearly a third of its calls were view\_canvas (about 23 self-reviews per drawing), and like Sol it never touched the set\_* tools.

## Cost and tokens

**Claude Fable 5 cost an estimated $160 for its seven drawings, roughly 20x GPT-5.6 Sol ($7.74), Grok 4.5 ($9.21), or Gemini 3.6 Flash ($12.87).** This shouldn't really be a surprise by now. Grok used by far the most tokens (34M) but stayed cheap because ~98% were cached reads at a fraction of the input rate, and Grok's rates are the lowest of the four. Gemini also leaned heavily on cached reads, so even at 27.7M tokens it stayed modest.

## Did the models actually improve?

Every view\_canvas re-scored the canvas against the target, so we can watch progress over a run. Two patterns held across both targets:

First, **they plateau early.** Claude Fable 5 reviewed its Mona Lisa 27 times, but its similarity was essentially flat after about the fifth review. Second, **in all eight target runs, the final drawing scored below the best the model reached mid-run.** GPT-5.6 Sol's Mona Lisa peaked at 0.352 SSIM and ended at 0.325; its Starry Night peaked at 0.179 and ended at 0.130. Gemini 3.6 Flash reviewed the most (about 23 times per drawing) and reached the highest peak of the whole batch, 0.449 on the Mona Lisa, then slid all the way back to 0.337 by the end. More reviewing did not translate into a better finish. The models kept editing past their own best performance.

## Objective similarity (target runs)

SSIM is 0 to 1, higher is closer to the target. RMSE is 0 to 255, lower is closer to the target.

ModelGPT-5.6 SolMona Lisa448m41s70.3250.35254.2Claude Fable 5Mona Lisa8618m58s270.2860.28956.9Grok 4.5Mona Lisa1064m30s50.1510.15295.6Gemini 3.6 FlashMona Lisa605m31s220.3370.44951.1GPT-5.6 SolStarry Night235m47s40.1300.17937.8Claude Fable 5Starry Night4311m30s120.1530.17640.9Grok 4.5Starry Night585m51s30.0390.039104.9Gemini 3.6 FlashStarry Night746m36s240.1720.19042.8

On raw SSIM, Gemini 3.6 Flash scored highest on both targets, on final score and on peak, though it also swung the most between its best frame and its final one. Gemini 3.6 Flash looked decent, but definitely does not look the closest to the target output. Very interesting that the raw score turned out to be higher though. As always, SSIM measures structural closeness, not how good the drawing looks to a person (see our take below).

## Method notes

- Four models, two scored targets plus five prompts each, 28 drawings total. All runs used the same colored-pencil toolset. Reasoning was set to high for every model.
- SSIM/RMSE are computed by resizing both images to 256x256 and comparing. They measure structural/pixel closeness, not artistic quality, and only apply to the target runs (prompts have no reference to score against).
- Wall-clock time includes the model's own thinking and tool round-trips.

## Our take

GPT-5.6 Sol was the runaway leader. Its Starry Night and its rose were our favorites of the whole batch, which is wild considering it drew them stroke by stroke on a blank canvas. It also beat the other two on detail in almost every drawing, the one exception being the elderly fisherman.

Grok 4.5 was basically garbage here. It rarely produced anything usable, and despite its frantic tool use I would not trust it to reliably understand or judge visual output yet.

Gemini 3.6 Flash was definitely better than Grok at a slight cost increase, but comparing the Flash model to frontier versions seems a little unfair. I'm definitely excited to see how Gemini 4 will perform. We also already knew this, but the token output speed is just so impressive.

Claude Fable 5 landed second on quality but at the far end on cost and time. For this task it was the least appealing trade-off of the three: much slower and far more expensive (about 20x the others) for output that did not keep up with Sol.
